~~~~text
● prisma dev can give us a disposable local PostgreSQL that only listens on this
  machine. The one catch is that it downloads its own code from npm at runtime,
  so I'm proposing we pin it to a fixed version.

  Nothing was started, migrated, installed or edited. I opened no .env* file and
  connected to no database. The branch is feature/us1-google-auth-community at
  52d84cc, and the tree is clean.

  What I found

  Can prisma dev give us an isolated local database?

  Yes, with conditions:
  - Engine: it runs PGlite, which is Postgres compiled to WebAssembly. The WASM
    files ship inside the package, so it doesn't download a native Postgres
    binary.
  - Network: the server listens on 127.0.0.1 only.
  - Connection URL: a standard postgres:// address, which is what our
    @prisma/adapter-pg client needs: postgres://postgres:postgres@localhost:<db-
    port>/template1?sslmode=disable&connection_limit=10&…. The postgres:postgres
    login is a fixed local default, not a real credential.
  - Where data lives: in a named server under your user profile. It stays until
    you remove it with prisma dev rm.
  - No servers exist yet. npx prisma dev ls shows an empty list.

  prisma dev downloads its own code

  The Prisma CLI doesn't use the @prisma/dev@0.24.17 already in our lockfile.
  Reading node_modules/prisma/build/cli.js, prisma dev runs npm install
  @prisma/cli-dev@latest --no-save into %TEMP%, refreshes it daily, and imports
  it from there. That's an unpinned download from outside the lockfile, which
  amounts to installing a tool.
  - This already happened once. When I ran prisma dev --help during CP-6 and
    prisma dev ls today, the CLI cached @prisma/cli-dev@latest (0.16.28, which
    depends on @prisma/dev@0.25.2) in %TEMP%\@prisma\. No server was started. I
    should have pointed out that download at the time.
  - It can be pinned. The CLI accepts a version as the first argument, so npx
    prisma dev @0.16.28 … installs exactly that version once, into its own cache
    folder. 0.16.28 is the current latest and was published on 2026-08-25.

  How shared databases are kept out

  - No database variables are set in the shell: I checked whether DATABASE_URL
    and TEST_DATABASE_URL exist, without printing values. Neither does.
  - Where the database URL comes from:
    - Prisma commands read it from process.env.DATABASE_URL in prisma.config.ts.
      That file first runs import "dotenv/config", which could load a repo env
      file pointing at Neon.
    - The tests read process.env.TEST_DATABASE_URL.
    - Vitest doesn't load env files into process.env.
  - dotenv 18 doesn't overwrite variables that are already set, unless
    DOTENV_OVERRIDE turns that on. DOTENV_PATH chooses which file it loads.
  - So I propose a small guard script, kept in the scratchpad and not in the
    repo, that wraps every database command:
    a. It accepts only postgres:// or postgresql:// URLs whose host is exactly
       localhost, 127.0.0.1 or ::1, with a numeric port. Anything else is
       refused before any command runs, including any Neon host and
       prisma+postgres://.
    b. For the wrapped command, it sets DATABASE_URL and TEST_DATABASE_URL to
       that verified URL. It also sets DOTENV_PATH to an empty scratchpad file,
       so no repo env file is loaded at all, forces DOTENV_OVERRIDE=false, and
       clears any DOTENV_CONFIG_* variables.
    c. It prints only host:port/database, never the full URL.
  - An identity check before migrating: through the guard, run SELECT version()
    with the pg driver we already have, to confirm we've reached the local
    PGlite server.

  Proposed commands

  Every command runs from ~/Documents/BorrowLoop. GUARD stands for
  node "<scratchpad>/local-db-guard.mjs" "<url>" --.

  #: 0
  Command: Create local-db-guard.mjs and an empty empty.env in the scratchpad
  Expected result: Nothing in the repo changes
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 1
  Command: npx prisma dev @0.16.28 --name borrowloop-test --detach
  Expected result: Needs your approval. Installs @prisma/cli-dev@0.16.28 once
    into %TEMP%, then starts a background server on 127.0.0.1 with default
    ports 51213, 51214 and 51215.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 2
  Command: npx prisma dev @0.16.28 ls
  Expected result: Lists borrowloop-test as running, with its
    postgres://…@localhost:<port>/template1… URL
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 3
  Command: GUARD node <scratchpad>/db-identity.mjs
  Expected result: Shows a PGlite or Postgres 17 version string, which
    confirms the local server
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 4
  Command: GUARD npx prisma migrate status
  Expected result: Reports 2 migrations not yet applied
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 5
  Command: GUARD npx prisma migrate deploy
  Expected result: Needs your approval. Applies 20261121210255_init and
    20261122120000_add_auth_account. It only applies existing migration files
    and doesn't need a shadow database.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 6
  Command: GUARD npx vitest run --no-file-parallelism prisma/account.test.ts
    src/lib/item-photos.test.ts
  Expected result: 7 passed, 0 skipped. Item-photos mocks Cloudinary, so there
    are no network calls.
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 7
  Command: GUARD npx vitest run --no-file-parallelism
  Expected result: Full suite: 283 passed, 0 skipped, if everything works
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: 8
  Command: npx prisma dev @0.16.28 stop borrowloop-test, then npx prisma dev
    @0.16.28 rm borrowloop-test
  Expected result: Stops the server and deletes its data. Teardown.

  I'd run tests with --no-file-parallelism because PGlite multiplexes everything
  over one connection, so the database test files shouldn't run at the same
  time.

  Limitations

  1. PGlite isn't a full Postgres server.
     - What it does verify: the migration SQL, the unique index, the foreign key
       and the cascade delete all run on real Postgres code.
     - What it doesn't: concurrent connections are multiplexed and transactions
       are serialized, so future concurrency tests (such as overlapping loan
       approvals) won't mean much here. It also says nothing about Neon's
       pooling or SSL.
  2. The 7 tests don't run the adapter itself against the database.
     prisma/account.test.ts uses Prisma directly. A test running
     handleLoginOrRegister with the real PrismaAdapter and a real client would
     be a new test file, which is a code change. It would need your approval as
     part of CP-7B.
  3. The prisma dev code sits outside the lockfile. Pinning to @0.16.28 makes it
     repeatable, but it's still a separate npm install into %TEMP%.
  4. Ports: if 51213–51215 are in use, prisma dev may pick others. The guard
     reads whatever URL ls reports, so that's handled.
  5. template1 database: prisma dev connects to template1, Postgres's built-in
     template database. That's harmless on a throwaway server, and it disappears
     with rm.
  6. Windows: this hasn't been tried on this machine yet. If --detach or PGlite
     misbehaves, step 1 or 2 is where it would show, before anything is
     migrated.

  Approvals I need for the next step

  - Step 1: install the pinned @prisma/cli-dev@0.16.28 into %TEMP% and start the
    borrowloop-test server.
  - Step 5: apply the two existing migrations to that local server only.
  - Optional: a real-database adapter test as described in limitation 2. This
    adds one test file.

  What was set up

  - Scratchpad scripts in ~\AppData\Local\Temp\claude\4f4b71b6a12f7
    04f\92908f77-c103-4207-a864-35c2207d85ca\scratchpad\local-db\, all outside
    the repo:
    - local-db-guard.mjs
    - db-identity.mjs, the read-only SELECT version() check, not run yet
    - empty.env, 0 bytes
  - What the guard enforces:
    - Only postgres:// or postgresql:// URLs, with a host of exactly localhost,
      127.0.0.1 or ::1 and an explicit numeric port.
    - The child process gets only DATABASE_URL and TEST_DATABASE_URL, set to the
      verified URL. DOTENV_PATH points at empty.env, DOTENV_OVERRIDE=false is
      forced, and other DOTENV_* and database URL variables are removed. --no-db
      mode removes all database URLs.
    - Commands run without a shell. My first version used one, and on Windows
      that joins arguments without escaping, so I removed it.
  - Guard tests:
    - All 8 bad URLs were refused before anything ran: a Neon host,
      localhost.evil.com, a URL with no port, prisma+postgres://, mysql://,
      10.0.0.5, 127.0.0.2, and an invalid URL.
    - All 3 local URLs were accepted.
    - Remote URLs and DOTENV_OVERRIDE=true set in the parent shell were
      overridden.
    - Loading dotenv/config inside the guard added 0 variables.
  - Pinned tool: @prisma/cli-dev@0.16.28 (with @prisma/dev@0.25.2) is installed
    in %TEMP%\@prisma\cli-dev@0.16.28.

  Why prisma dev doesn't fit

  - What it started: the borrowloop-test server came up. ls showed TCP on
    localhost:51214, database template1.
  - What netstat showed:

  | Port  | Purpose                             | Bound to
             |
  |-------|-------------------------------------|-------------------------------
  ------------|
  | 51213 | HTTP query API (prisma+postgres://) | 0.0.0.0 and [::], meaning
  every interface |
  | 51214 | Postgres TCP                        | 127.0.0.1
             |
  | 51215 | Shadow database                     | 127.0.0.1
             |
  | 51216 | Streams                             | 127.0.0.1
             |

  - Cause: in the package code, the HTTP server calls listen(port) with no host.
    The CLI only offers --port, --db-port and --shadow-db-port, with no host
    option. Its API key only encodes the server's name and ports, so it isn't a
    real secret.
  - What I did: stopped the server with prisma dev @0.16.28 stop
    borrowloop-test. Nothing now listens on ports 51210–51219. The server's data
    folder still exists, because I didn't remove it.

  Proposed alternative

  Run PGlite's own socket server from a small scratchpad script.
  - Uses packages already in our lockfile: @electric-sql/pglite@0.4.3 and
    @electric-sql/pglite-socket@0.1.3, both installed as Prisma dependencies.
    Nothing new is downloaded.
  - Setup: new PGLiteSocketServer({ db, host: "127.0.0.1", port: 54329,
    maxConnections: 10 }), with the data folder in the scratchpad.
    - No HTTP API, so the only open port is one Postgres port on 127.0.0.1. I'd
      confirm that with netstat before running any query.
  - Same engine as prisma dev, so the same limits apply: one connection
    multiplexed, so tests run with --no-file-parallelism. That's fine for
    constraint, foreign-key and cascade tests, but not for concurrency testing.
  - Teardown: stop the script and delete its scratchpad folder.

  If you'd rather keep prisma dev, the only way to protect port 51213 is a
  Windows Firewall rule blocking it, which changes system settings. I don't
  recommend that.

  Decisions for you

  1. Approve the PGlite socket server in place of prisma dev. Then I'd start it,
     confirm with netstat that it listens only on 127.0.0.1:54329, and run the
     read-only SELECT version() through the guard. No migrations.
  2. Clean up prisma dev's leftovers: run npx prisma dev @0.16.28 rm
     borrowloop-test to delete its data folder (it only holds an empty
     database), and optionally remove the two cached %TEMP%\@prisma\cli-dev*
     folders.

      version.

    - The generated Prisma client from createPrismaClient(TEST_DATABASE_URL)
      over PrismaPg.

      option, and our real keepServerIssuedToken and exposeSessionUserId
      callbacks.
  - Simulated parts: the Google profile and token response that Auth.js would
    receive after a successful OAuth exchange. Google isn't contacted, and the
    consent screen and redirects aren't exercised. The file header says so.
  - Isolation and cleanup:
    - Each run uses a random prefix (cp7b-<8 hex>) for every Google ID and
      email, with @example.test addresses.
    - beforeAll, afterEach and afterAll delete rows matching this run's prefix,
      by user email or by the account's Google ID, so cleanup runs even when an
      assertion fails.
    - afterAll also disconnects the client.
  - Skipping: describe.skipIf(!TEST_DATABASE_URL), the same pattern as
    prisma/account.test.ts. A comment warns never to point it at Neon.

~~~~

**Annotation (my approach):** I wanted some tests against a real database, not only mocks, but we weren't going to use the shared Neon database for that. While setting up the local tests, Claude noticed that Prisma dev had an HTTP interface listening on more than just localhost. We stopped it and switched to a PGlite-based local test server. That was a good catch, and it had nothing to do with the feature code itself.

### Database-backed authorization and membership checks

~~~~text
  From the docs:
  - PRD US-1 lists as ⚠ Needs team decision: how users join (invite code, admin
    approval, or both), whether users can belong to several communities, and how
    the first admin is created.
  - Architecture D4 (community joining) and D15 are undecided.
  - Architecture §6: scope queries in the query itself, never trust
    client-supplied IDs, and return not-found for communities the user can't
    access.
  - Security §4: check membership on each request rather than caching it in the
    session, so removed members lose access immediately.
  - Testing §2 and §3: test unauthenticated, cross-user and cross-community
    access, and run authorization tests against real database rows, not mocked
    guards.

  5. Membership authorization design

  - Finding a membership: db.membership.findUnique({ where: {
    userId_communityId: { userId, communityId } }, select: { role: true } }),
    using the schema's unique key.
    - userId always comes from the user passed in, which requireUser derives
      from the session. It's never a route or form value.
    - communityId has already been Zod-validated by authorizeCommunity.
  - No status column, so "has a row" means active (recommended, D-2):
    - A row maps to { role, status: "ACTIVE" }. No row maps to null.
    - The pure rules stay unchanged. PENDING, SUSPENDED and REMOVED remain in
      the type for later work, such as US-8 moderation.
    - Removing a member means deleting the row. Lookups happen on every request,
      and nothing is cached in the JWT or under a shared cache key, so access
      ends on the next request, as security §4 requires.
  - Communities that don't exist and non-members both get null and so not_found.
    This doesn't reveal whether another community exists.
  - Malformed IDs or extra fields such as userId are rejected by the strict Zod
    schema, giving not_found without a query.
  - Member versus admin:

  | Request level | Not a member | MEMBER    | ADMIN |
  |---------------|--------------|-----------|-------|
  | member        | not_found    | allow     | allow |
  | admin         | not_found    | forbidden | allow |
    forbidden maps to 403, which CP-10 will deliver as a real status code.
  - The /communities page:
    - Reads the user's own communities with membership.findMany({ where: {
      userId: user.id }, select: { role: true, community: { select: { id, name,
      slug, description } } }, orderBy: { community: { name: "asc" } } }).
    - Zero communities: an empty state saying the user isn't in any community
      yet. The wording depends on D-1.
    - One or more: a list showing each community's name, description, and an
      "Admin" badge where role is ADMIN.
    - Several communities are allowed: the schema permits it, and URLs carry the
      community ID, so there's no "current community" to switch.
    - Links to /c/[communityId] are added in CP-9, when that page exists, so
      CP-8 doesn't produce links that 404.
    - It stays inside the existing <Suspense>, with no "use cache", because the
      data is per user.
    - All text is rendered by React, so it's escaped.

  6. Test strategy

  Unit tests (no database):
  - memberships.test.ts:
    - Queries by the exact compound key and selects only role.
    - A row maps to an ACTIVE snapshot with the right role, and no row maps to
      null.
    - listCommunitiesForUser filters by the given user only, maps fields,
      returns [] for zero, and sorts by name.
  - community-access.test.ts:
    - Calls requireUser with the callback path and redirects when signed out
      (AC2).
    - Passes the session user's ID to the lookup, never anything from params.
    - Invalid or extra params give not_found with no lookup.
    - Covers member/admin × non-member/MEMBER/ADMIN, the full table above.
  - communities.test.ts:
    - Covers the zero, one and several cases.
    - Shows the admin badge only for ADMIN.
    - Escapes community names.
    - Shows only the communities returned for this user.

  Database tests, prisma/membership.test.ts (run through the guard against local
  PGlite):
  - Fixtures: unique names per run, with users A, B and C and communities X and
    Y.
    - A is a MEMBER of X and Y, and B is the ADMIN of X.
    - C has no memberships.
  - Real lookups and the real guard: authorizeCommunity with
    createMembershipLookup, no mocks.
    - A on X at member level is allowed. A on X at admin level is forbidden. B
      on X at admin level is allowed.
    - C on X is not_found, and so is A on a nonexistent cuid.
    - An invalid ID and params with an extra userId are both not_found.
  - Isolation: listCommunitiesForUser returns X and Y for A, only X for B, and
    [] for C. No user sees a community they don't belong to.
  - Revocation: after deleting A's X membership row, the next check is
    not_found.
  - Cascade: deleting a community removes its memberships.
  - Cleanup: in afterEach, by the run's slug and email prefix, so it runs even
    when a test fails. Sequential runs with --no-file-parallelism.

  Full validation each checkpoint: guarded full suite, a no-database run
  (CI-like, with the database tests skipping), lint, typegen, tsc, a build with
  empty database URLs, and git diff --check.

  7. Decisions I need from you

  #: D-1
  Decision: How users join a community. AC3 says "when they join", but the PRD
    and architecture D4 leave the mechanism to the team
  Options: (a) No join UI in US-1: memberships come from the seed or an admin
    script, and "join" means "become a member"<br>(b) Invite code: needs a
    Community.joinCode column, a migration and Aarushi's review<br>(c) Signed,
    expiring invite link (an HMAC with AUTH_SECRET): no schema change, but it
    can't be revoked without rotating the secret, and it needs a way to create
    links<br>(d) Open join by community ID: rejected, because anyone could
    join any community, which defeats the point of community isolation
  Recommendation: (a) for CP-8, recording D4 as "team decision pending". If
    the team decides self-join belongs in US-1, add it as a separate CP-8c,
    preferably (b) with an approved migration
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: D-2
  Decision: What Membership without status means
  Options: Row means ACTIVE (no schema change), or add status (needs a
    migration)
  Recommendation: Row means ACTIVE. No current acceptance criterion needs
    suspension, and it can be revisited in US-8
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: D-3
  Decision: Community URLs: ID or slug
  Options: /c/[communityId] (cuid, matches the existing Zod schema, the proxy
    matcher and the architecture docs) or /c/[slug]
  Recommendation: ID, which keeps CP-PRE and CP-10 as they are
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  #: D-4
  Decision: How the first admin is created
  Options: Seed or a one-off script (outside the app), or a UI
  Recommendation: Seed or script. There's no community-creation UI in US-1

  schema and pure access rules.

  key.

     - Return null when no membership exists.
     - Provide listCommunitiesForUser using the authenticated user's database
  ID.
     - Ensure all queries are scoped by userId.

  2. Create src/server/auth/community-access.ts.

  decisions.

  Change: 13 database tests, skipped unless TEST_DATABASE_URL is set

  2. How database-backed authorization works

  - Finding a membership: membership.findUnique({ where: { userId_communityId: {
    userId, communityId } }, select: { role: true } }).
    - A row maps to { role, status: "ACTIVE" }, and no row maps to null
      (decision D-2).
    - The existing pure rules turn that into allow, not_found or forbidden,
      unchanged.
  - The community list: membership.findMany({ where: { userId: user.id }, …
    orderBy: community name }). It returns { id, name, slug, description, role }
    for each community.
  - Lazy creation is kept: getClient() runs only inside a query, never when the
    lookup is created or the module imported. The build with empty database URLs
    succeeded.
  - The role type stays in sync with the schema: MembershipRoleContract asserts
    that Prisma's CommunityRole equals MembershipRole in both directions. A
    temporary probe, since deleted, confirmed that a missing role and an extra
    role ("OWNER") each fail tsc with TS2344.

  3. How session identity is enforced

  - getCommunityAccess calls requireUser(callbackPath) first. Without a valid
    session it redirects to sign-in, and no lookup runs.
  - The lookup gets user.id from that session, the permanent database ID since
    CP-7.
  - authorizeCommunity checks params against the strict Zod schema before any
    query. A client-supplied userId or role, an invalid or missing ID, or
    non-object params all return not_found, and the database tests confirm 0
    lookups in those cases.
  - Membership is read from the database on every call and is never stored in
    the JWT. It isn't cached either; that will matter in CP-8b and CP-9 under
    cacheComponents.

  4. Member, admin and cross-community results (real database)

  Fixtures are fresh for every test, with names unique to each run:
  - alice is a MEMBER of Alpha and Beta.
  - bob is the ADMIN of Alpha.
  - carol has no memberships.

  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┬────────┐
  │                              Check                              │ Result │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ Lookup: alice/Alpha gives MEMBER/ACTIVE, bob/Alpha gives        │        │
  │ ADMIN/ACTIVE; bob/Beta, carol/Alpha and alice on a nonexistent  │ ✓      │
  │ ID give null                                                    │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ Member level: alice on Alpha and Beta, and bob on Alpha, are    │ ✓      │
  │ allowed                                                         │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ Member level: carol on Alpha and Beta, and bob on Beta (admin   │ ✓      │
  │ of Alpha, not a member of Beta), are not_found                  │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ A nonexistent community gets the same not_found as an           │ ✓      │
  │ inaccessible one                                                │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ Admin level: bob on Alpha is allowed, alice on Alpha and Beta   │ ✓      │
  │ is forbidden, carol on Alpha and bob on Beta are not_found      │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ Invalid ID, empty ID, extra userId, extra role, {}, null: all   │ ✓      │
  │ not_found with 0 database lookups                               │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ The lookup always receives the authenticated user's ID          │ ✓      │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ listCommunitiesForUser: alice gets Alpha and Beta (MEMBER,      │ ✓      │
  │ sorted), bob gets Alpha (ADMIN), carol gets []                  │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ Immediate revocation: after deleting alice's Alpha row, the     │        │
  │ next check is not_found, Beta is still allowed, and her list    │ ✓      │
  │ shows only Beta                                                 │        │
  ├─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┼────────┤
  │ Cascade: deleting Beta removes its memberships, alice gets      │ ✓      │
  │ not_found for Beta, and her list shows only Alpha               │        │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┴────────┘

  Checking the tests can fail:
  - I temporarily changed the lookup to findFirst({ where: { communityId } }),
    which ignores the user.
  - 5 database tests failed, including "hides communities the user doesn't
    belong to" and the admin-only checks.
  - memberships.ts was restored byte for byte.
  - The failing run left User=0 Community=0 Membership=0, so cleanup in
    afterEach works on failure.

  7. Remaining concerns

  1. AC3 is not complete. CP-8a provides real membership checks, but how users
     join (D-1) is still open, and community-scoped listings come in CP-9. Per
     your instruction, AC3 stays unchecked.
  2. Nothing calls getCommunityAccess yet. CP-9's /c/[communityId] page and
     CP-10's proxy will use it. It's covered by unit tests now and will get real
     page-level testing then.
  3. /communities still shows the placeholder. That's CP-8b, which will use
     listCommunitiesForUser.
  4. No suspension status (D-2): removing a member means deleting the row.
     Suspending or reinstating a member would need a status column later,
     possibly in US-8.
  5. communityIdSchema is still loose ([A-Za-z0-9_-]{1,64}). It accepts cuids,
     so I haven't tightened it.
  6. PGlite limits (unchanged): real Postgres behavior for constraints and
     cascades, but one multiplexed connection, so no concurrency or
     Neon-specific coverage.

  Suggested commit message: feat(communities): back community authorization with
  Prisma memberships

~~~~

**Annotation (my approach):** I kept membership checks as their own step. Signing in with Google isn't enough to see a community's data; the user also has to be a member with the right status. The database tests helped check that suspended users and people outside a community couldn't get access just because they had a session.

### Community list and member-scoped item listings

~~~~text
● All 15 component tests pass, and tsc and lint are clean. The IDE error was

● /communities now shows the signed-in user's real communities from the database
  instead of the placeholder. All 15 component tests pass, and the full suite
  passed against the local database: 333 passed, 0 failed, 0 skipped. I didn't
  open the page in a browser with a real signed-in session; the tests render the
  component directly. Everything is uncommitted.

  I didn't touch the schema, migrations, auth or membership logic, page.tsx,
  packages or env files, and connected to no external database. Database runs
  went through local-db-guard.mjs to 127.0.0.1:54329.

  1. Files modified

  Nothing changed in the repo since 8ba35c2 before I started.

  File: src/app/(app)/communities/communities-content.tsx
  Change: Calls listCommunitiesForUser after requireUser. The placeholder is
    replaced with an empty state or a list, using a new CommunityCard
    component with an Admin badge (52 lines changed)
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: src/app/(app)/communities/communities.test.ts
  Change: Mocks listCommunitiesForUser. Keeps the original 6 tests, adapting
    the one that checked the placeholder, and adds 9 (155 lines changed)

  page.tsx is unchanged: its <Suspense> boundary and "Checking your session…"
  fallback still wrap the content.

  2. How the page gets the user's communities

  1. requireUser("/communities") runs first. Without a valid session it
     redirects to sign-in, and the list query never runs; a test checks this.
  2. Then listCommunitiesForUser({ id: user.id }), from CP-8a. The ID comes only
     from the session, the query filters on userId, and results are sorted by
     name.
  3. Not cached: there's no "use cache". The bundled caching guide says that for
     per-request database data you should "not use "use cache"" and stream it
     inside <Suspense>, which is what happens here. The component also reads the
     session cookie first, so the route renders per request. The build shows ◐
     /communities, partial prerender: the static shell is prerendered and the
     user-specific part streams.
  4. Everything runs on the server. No client component, no API route, and
     community IDs and slugs aren't sent in the HTML.

  3. What the user sees

  The signed-in name and the Sign out button are unchanged, followed by a "Your
  communities" heading (<h2 id="communities-heading">).

  ┌─────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │    Case     │                       What renders                        │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │             │ A dashed box: "You're not a member of any community yet." │
  │ No          │  and "Communities you join will appear here." This        │
  │ communities │ doesn't promise any particular way of joining, since D-1  │
  │             │ is still open. There's no <ul>.                           │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ One         │ A <ul aria-labelledby="communities-heading"> with one     │
  │ community   │ card: the name as an <h3> and the description as a        │
  │             │ paragraph if there is one.                                │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Several     │ One card each, in the order returned (alphabetical).      │
  │ communities │                                                           │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ Admin role  │ A green "Admin" badge next to the name, only where the    │
  │             │ user's role is ADMIN.                                     │
  ├─────────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
  │ No          │ The paragraph is left out.                                │
  │ description │                                                           │
  └─────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  There are no links to /c/[id] (they arrive in CP-9) and no IDs or slugs in the
  markup. All text goes through React, so it's escaped.

  4. Test results

  Component tests: 15 passed (15).
  - Session handling:
    - requireUser is called with /communities.
    - A signed-out visitor is redirected before any database lookup.
    - The list query gets { id: session user ID } exactly once.
    - The name and sign-out button render, but not the user ID.
    - The user's name is escaped.
    - A missing name falls back to "BorrowLoop member".
  - The list:
    - The empty state shows, the placeholder text is gone, and there's no <ul>.
    - One community shows its name and description.
    - Two communities show in order, with exactly 2 <li>.
    - A missing description leaves out the paragraph.
    - "Admin" appears exactly once, on the ADMIN card only.
    - Only the communities returned for this user render.
    - There are no <a> links or community IDs.
    - A <script> in a name and an <img onerror> in a description render as
      escaped text.
  - Sign-out: the action still redirects to /sign-in.

  I fixed one problem in my own test while writing it. The escaping assertion
  first failed because the only literal <script> in the output was React's own
  form-replay helper, which it adds for the sign-out form. The page itself
  escaped correctly, so I narrowed the assertion to the injected
  <script>alert(…).

  Database tests: I added no new fixtures. CP-8a's prisma/membership.test.ts
  already covers listCommunitiesForUser against real rows: several communities
  sorted with roles, an empty list, isolation, revocation and cascade. All 13 of
  those tests passed again.

  6. Remaining issues

  1. No live end-to-end check yet. The page hasn't been loaded with a real
     signed-in session against seeded memberships. Doing that needs either your
     real Google sign-in against the local database or a test-only signed
     session, which is the HTTP-check approach planned for CP-9 and CP-10. The
     data access behind it is covered by the database tests.
  2. AC3 is still incomplete: the joining mechanism (D-1) and community-scoped
     listings (CP-9) are both outstanding.
  3. Placeholder wording: "Communities you join will appear here" is
     deliberately neutral until D-1 is decided. It may need changing once the
     joining mechanism exists.
  4. No pagination: the list returns every community the user belongs to. That's
     fine at v1's scale.
  5. Fallback text: the <Suspense> fallback still says "Checking your session…"
     while the list loads. It's accurate, but it could mention loading
     communities in a later cleanup.

  Suggested commit message: feat(communities): show the signed-in user's
  communities

> We are continuing BorrowLoop US-1, moving directly from CP-8b into CP-9.

  Current branch: feature/us1-google-auth-community
  Current committed HEAD: 8ba35c2

  IMPORTANT:
  CP-8b is implemented and fully tested but NOT committed. Preserve those two
  modified files. We will review and commit CP-8b and CP-9 together if the
  combined implementation passes.

  GOAL:
  Implement community-scoped item browsing so an authenticated user can view
  listings belonging only to a community they are authorized to access.

  PHASE 1 — EXPLORE AND PLAN

  1. Inspect the current Item, Community, Membership, Availability, and related
  Prisma models.
  2. Review US-1 Issue #6, the PRD, architecture, security and testing
  requirements.
  3. Inspect existing item listing code, routes, components and relevant tests.
  4. Review getCommunityAccess and listCommunitiesForUser from CP-8a.
  5. Determine the minimum implementation needed to satisfy the
  community-scoped listings portion of US-1.
  6. Identify existing item visibility, availability or status rules that must
  be preserved.
  7. Avoid implementing item creation, editing, borrowing or other user
  stories.
  8. Identify any unresolved architectural decisions before implementation.

  PHASE 2 — IMPLEMENT

  If the existing schema and requirements are sufficient, proceed without
  waiting for another approval.

  1. Implement a protected /c/[communityId] page using the existing route
  conventions.
  2. Resolve the authenticated user on the server.
  3. Call getCommunityAccess with member-level access before querying or
  rendering community listings.
  4. Return not-found behavior for users who do not belong to the requested
  community, without exposing its existence.
  5. Query items with the communityId restriction inside the Prisma query
  itself, never by fetching all items and filtering afterward.
  6. Apply existing item visibility and availability rules where required by
  the schema and PRD.
  7. Display the community's name and its authorized listings.
  8. Provide an accessible empty state if the community has no visible
  listings.
  9. Update /communities to link each community card to its protected detail
  page, but only once that route exists and is tested.
  10. Keep database access server-side and user-specific data uncached.

  TESTING

  Add focused unit and database-gated integration tests proving:

  - Unauthenticated users are redirected.
  - A member can view listings in their own community.
  - An admin can view listings in their own community.
  - A user cannot access a community they don't belong to.
  - Membership in one community does not grant access to another.
  - Items from community B never appear in community A's results.
  - Invalid community IDs are rejected.
  - Removing a membership revokes access on the next request.
  - Empty communities render correctly.
  - No client-supplied userId or role can override authorization.

  Run the full suite against our isolated local PGlite database, verifying its
  localhost-only listener first and using local-db-guard.mjs for all database
  operations.

  Also run the CI-style suite without a database, lint, Next typegen,
  TypeScript, production build with empty database URLs, and git diff --check.

  SCOPE LIMITS

  - Do not implement the joining or invitation mechanism; D-1 is still
  unresolved.
  - Do not implement item creation, editing, loan requests, or other user
  stories.
  - Do not change Prisma models or migrations without stopping for approval.
  - Do not implement unrelated admin functionality.
  - Do not change shared authentication logic without stopping for approval.
  - Do not access Neon, real credentials, or repository .env files.
  - Do not install packages.
  - Do not perform any Git mutations.
  - Preserve the existing uncommitted CP-8b work.

  If a major unresolved requirement prevents a safe implementation, stop after
  explaining the blocker and your recommended solution.

  FINAL REPORT

  Summarize all CP-8b and CP-9 changes together, list files modified, explain
  the authorization and listing queries, report exact test counts, and identify
  remaining acceptance criteria.

  Leave everything uncommitted for my review.

  What I settled during exploration

  - No listing code existed before this. The existing schema was enough, with no
    migration needed.
  - Visibility: only PUBLISHED items are shown. DRAFT isn't published,
    PENDING_REVIEW is held for admin review (the PRD's proposed default for
    flagged listings), and ARCHIVED is retired.
  - Availability and borrowing rules belong to US-3 and US-5 and don't affect
    browsing, so I didn't apply them here.
  - What a listing shows: title, category, condition and description. No owner
    information or photos (see risks).

  How authorization and the listings query work

  On /c/[communityId], in CommunityListingsContent, inside <Suspense>:
  1. It waits for params, which is runtime data in Next 16.
  2. getCommunityAccess({ params, level: "member", callbackPath }), from CP-8a:
     - requireUser redirects a signed-out visitor to sign-in, before any
       database read. The proxy already sends a real 307 to visitors with no
       session cookie.
     - The strict Zod schema rejects invalid IDs and any extra userId or role.
     - The Prisma membership lookup uses the session user's ID.
     - callbackPath is /c/<id> only when the ID passes validation, otherwise
       /communities.
  3. Any result other than allow calls notFound(). That covers non-members,
     nonexistent communities and invalid IDs alike, so the page never reveals
     whether a community exists. Listings are never read in those cases.
  4. Only then does it call getCommunityWithListings({ id: user.id },
     access.communityId), using the validated ID from the access result rather
     than the raw route value. If that returns null, say because the membership
     was removed between the check and the read, it also calls notFound().

  The listings query checks membership and filters by community in the database
  itself, as a second line of defense:
  community.findFirst({
    where: { id: communityId, memberships: { some: { userId: user.id } } },
    select: { id, name, description,
      items: { where: { status: "PUBLISHED" }, select: { id, title, description,
  category, condition },
               orderBy: [{ createdAt: "desc" }, { id: "asc" }] } },
  })
  Items come only through that community's own relation, so other communities'
  items can't be returned. Nothing loads all items and filters afterwards.

  Caching: there's no "use cache", the data is per user and read on every
  request, and everything runs on the server. The build shows ◐
  /c/[communityId], a partial prerender of the static shell with the content
  streamed.

  The page itself shows the community name as <h1>, its description, and a
  "Listings" <h2> with a <ul aria-labelledby> of cards: title, "Category ·
  Condition", and description. With no listings it shows "No listings in this
  community yet." All text is escaped.

  Other risks:
  1. The not-found status is 200, not 404. Because the page streams, notFound()
     shows the not-found page but the HTTP status stays 200, as the CP-4 finding
     predicted. Nothing leaks. CP-10's proxy gate will add the real 404.
  2. No live end-to-end check yet. Both pages are tested through rendering and
     real database queries, but not with a real signed-in session in a browser.
  3. Fixed page title: it's "Community · BorrowLoop" rather than the community's
     name. Using the name would mean a second authorized query in
     generateMetadata; I'd leave it for now.
  4. Listings show no owner or photos yet. Showing photos means configuring
     Cloudinary images for next/image, or allowing a plain <img> (ESLint
     currently flags that). That fits better with US-2.
  5. No pagination: each page loads every published item in the community.
     That's fine at v1's scale.

  Suggested commit message for both together: feat(communities): list members'
  communities and show community-scoped listings

~~~~

**Annotation (my approach):** Once membership was working, we added the actual community list and community-specific listings. I was mainly checking that someone in one community couldn't see another community's items. That mattered more to me than whether the page looked finished, because it was one of the main requirements for US-1.


## 8. Real HTTP 403/404, not just a page-level guard

### Proxy feasibility, production HTTP status checks, and authorization

**Original Claude Code transcript — `BorrowLoop-US1-Claude-Session.txt`, lines 7,967–9,322**

~~~~text
> We are beginning CP-10 of BorrowLoop US-1.

  Current branch: feature/us1-google-auth-community
  Current HEAD: b5be727

  CP-8 and CP-9 are committed and pushed. The last full local PostgreSQL test
  run passed 361 tests.

  GOAL:
  Implement reliable HTTP authorization enforcement for protected community and
  admin routes, satisfying US-1's requirement that non-admins receive HTTP
  403.

  PHASE 1 — EXPLORE AND VERIFY FEASIBILITY

  1. Review GitHub Issue #6 and its acceptance criteria.
  2. Inspect proxy.ts, matchCommunityPath, getCommunityAccess, existing
  authorization guards, and HTTP response helpers.
  3. Inspect the installed Next.js 16.4.6 documentation for proxy runtime
  behavior.
  4. Determine whether the configured proxy runtime supports our Prisma 7
  PostgreSQL client and Auth.js session handling.
  5. Investigate whether server-side notFound() inside Suspense can return an
  actual HTTP 404, or whether streaming necessarily produces a 200.
  6. Identify the safest supported way to return real HTTP 403 and 404
  responses without exposing community existence.
  7. Inspect whether admin routes currently exist and determine the smallest
  meaningful admin route needed to test US-1 AC4.
  8. Avoid introducing experimental Next.js features or unsupported runtime
  assumptions.

  PHASE 2 — IMPLEMENT, IF THE DESIGN IS SUPPORTED

  If you identify a supported and secure implementation, proceed.

  Requirements:

  - Unauthenticated users must redirect to sign-in.
  - Nonmembers must not be able to discover other communities.
  - Active MEMBER users must receive HTTP 403 when accessing their community's
  admin page.
  - Active ADMIN users must be permitted to access their community's admin
  page.
  - Users who do not belong to a community must receive HTTP 404.
  - Validate all community IDs with the existing Zod schema.
  - Resolve identity from the authenticated server session only.
  - Check current membership from PostgreSQL, not from a cached JWT role.
  - Preserve existing community browsing and listings behavior.
  - Keep the admin page minimal; do not implement moderation or other user
  stories.
  - Ensure denial responses do not leak sensitive information.

  If database-backed authorization in proxy.ts is unsupported or unsafe, do NOT
  force it. Propose a supported alternative, explain its HTTP status behavior,
  and stop for my approval if a real 403/404 cannot be guaranteed.

  PHASE 3 — TEST

  Add tests covering:

  - Signed-out visitors.
  - MEMBER versus ADMIN permissions.
  - Nonmembers and nonexistent communities.
  - Invalid community IDs.
  - Cross-community isolation.
  - Immediate membership revocation.
  - Actual HTTP response status codes, not merely mocked authorization
  decisions.

  Where feasible, use a local HTTP integration test to verify the real status
  codes.

  Run the full test suite against our isolated local PGlite database, verifying
  that it listens only on 127.0.0.1:54329 and using local-db-guard.mjs for
  every database operation.

  Also run the CI-like test suite, lint, Next typegen, TypeScript, production
  build with empty database URLs, and git diff --check.

  RESTRICTIONS:

  - Do not access shared Neon or real Google credentials.
  - Do not read or modify .env files.
  - Do not change the Prisma schema or migrations.
  - Do not implement community joining yet.
  - Do not implement unrelated admin features.
  - Do not install packages without approval.
  - Do not perform Git staging, commits, pushes, merges, resets, or branch
  changes.
  - Leave all changes uncommitted.

  FINAL REPORT:

  1. Whether Prisma authorization in the Next.js proxy is supported.
  2. The authorization design selected and why.
  3. Files changed.
  4. Evidence that actual HTTP 403/404 responses work.
  5. Exact unit, integration, and full-suite test results.
  6. Remaining US-1 acceptance criteria.

  Stop if the framework cannot reliably satisfy the HTTP status requirements
  using the proposed architecture. I will review the findings before approving
  an alternative.

● CP-10 is done: non-admin members now get a real HTTP 403 on their community's
  admin page, which is AC4. Non-members, nonexistent communities and invalid IDs
  get a real 404, and signed-out visitors get a 307 to sign-in. I confirmed
  this with real requests against next start (production build) and the local
  database: 25 of 25 checks passed.

  The full suite against the local database passed: 414 passed, 0 failed, 0
  skipped. Everything is uncommitted. There were no schema, migration, package,
  env or Git changes, and every database command went through
  local-db-guard.mjs.

  1. Can the proxy do Prisma-backed authorization?

  Yes, it works, with one caveat from the Next.js docs.
  - It runs on Node.js: proxy.md says "Proxy defaults to using the Node.js
    runtime" (line 257), which is what Prisma 7 with pg needs.
  - It builds and runs: the production build accepted a proxy that imports
    server-only modules, Auth.js (getCurrentUser and auth()) and the Prisma
    client, with empty database URLs. The real server then ran it correctly.
  - Pages can't produce real 403/404: loading.md says "Streaming page responses
    begin with a 200 status code" and "Run the corresponding check before
    streaming starts to return a 404, 403, or 401." That rules out notFound()
    inside <Suspense> for real status codes. The proxy runs before streaming.
  - The caveat: the authentication guide advises keeping proxy checks
    cookie-only and avoiding "database checks to prevent performance issues",
    because the proxy runs on every route, including prefetches. That's
    performance advice, not a statement that it doesn't work. I limited the
    cost:
    - The database check runs only on /c/* paths.
    - It's one lookup by the (userId, communityId) unique key.
    - Every other path keeps the cookie-only check, and a test confirms no
      session or database work happens there.

  2. The design I chose, and why

  The proxy(request) flow:
  1. Public paths pass through. With no session cookie, the request is
     redirected to sign-in (307). Same as before.
  2. matchCommunityPath (from CP-PRE) classifies the path:
     - Not a community path: pass through, with no database work.
     - A malformed path (an invalid ID, an encoded separator like %2F, an empty
       segment): 404, with no session or database work.
  3. getCurrentUser() verifies the JWT. An invalid or expired session is
     redirected to sign-in, which fixes the earlier "forged cookie gets 200"
     gap.
  4. authorizeCommunity runs with createMembershipLookup():
     - The community ID goes through the strict Zod schema again.
     - The lookup uses the session user's ID.
     - Membership is read from PostgreSQL on every request and is never in the
       JWT.
  5. Allow passes through. Forbidden gives a 403 page. Not found gives a 404
     page.

  Denial pages (accessDeniedPage) are fixed HTML that takes only the decision,
  so they can't include the community name, IDs or whether a community exists.
  They're sent with Cache-Control: no-store and X-Robots-Tag: noindex. The 404
  body is byte-identical for non-members and nonexistent communities; the HTTP
  check compares them.

  The pages still check on their own, so a matcher mistake can't expose data.
  The listings page uses getCommunityAccess(member). The new admin page uses
  getCommunityAccess(admin) and then getAdministeredCommunity, a query that
  requires an ADMIN membership row. If a member somehow reaches the page, it
  shows only a fixed "Only community admins can open this page." notice.

  The admin page is minimal: the community name plus "Moderation and membership
  tools will appear here." The tools themselves belong to US-8.

  A bug I found and fixed, which predates CP-10:
  - What I saw: the first live run with a valid session logged Next.js
    encountered the unstable value crypto.getRandomValues() while prerendering
    on /communities, /c/[id] and /c/[id]/admin.
  - Cause: Cache Components doesn't allow random values during prerendering.
    Auth.js generates some during the session check, most likely for its CSRF
    token.
  - Fix: await connection() at the top of the three page content components,
    which is the documented fix. I didn't touch the shared session.ts, because
    getCurrentUser also runs in the proxy.
  - Result: 0 such errors in the final run.
  - Note: this affected /communities from CP-8b on. The pages still rendered
    correctly, but every signed-in request logged the error.
~~~~

**Annotation (my approach):** We came back to the 403 problem from the beginning of the session and tested it against a production build. The checks showed 403 for a member without admin access, 404 for a non-member, and 307 for someone signed out. I wanted those actual HTTP responses because a unit test or an error message on the screen wouldn't prove the same thing.


## 9. Community joining and final verification

### Joining workflow, action validation, and debugging an unstable test server

**Original Claude Code transcript — `BorrowLoop-US1-Claude-Session.txt`, lines 9,323–10,561**

~~~~text
> We are implementing the final missing functional requirement of BorrowLoop
  US-1: open community joining.

  REPOSITORY:
  ~\Documents\BorrowLoop

  BRANCH:
  feature/us1-google-auth-community

  EXPECTED HEAD:
  eae6e1d

  DECISION:
  For the initial version of BorrowLoop, communities are open to authenticated
  users. Users can discover existing communities and join them without an
  invitation.

  This is an intentionally simple product decision to satisfy US-1's joining
  acceptance criterion. We are NOT implementing private communities, invite
  codes, community creation, or approval workflows.

  GOAL:
  Allow a signed-in user to discover an existing community, join it as a
  MEMBER, and immediately access only that community's published listings.

  PHASE 1 — EXPLORE

  1. Confirm the branch, HEAD, and clean working tree using read-only Git
  commands.
  2. Review US-1 Issue #6 and the relevant sections of docs/PRD.md,
  docs/architecture.md, docs/security.md, and docs/testing.md.
  3. Inspect the existing Prisma Community and Membership models and their
  unique constraints.
  4. Inspect /communities, listCommunitiesForUser, requireUser, existing server
  actions, and community authorization.
  5. Determine the smallest implementation that fits our Next.js 16, Auth.js,
  Prisma 7, and cacheComponents architecture.
  6. Check for conflicts with existing project decisions about community
  visibility or joining.

  If the repository explicitly requires invitation-only or approval-based
  communities, STOP and report the conflict before implementing.

  PHASE 2 — IMPLEMENT

  1. Add a simple Discover Communities section to /communities.
  2. List existing communities the signed-in user has not joined, showing their
  names and optional descriptions.
  3. Add a Join Community button for each available community.
  4. Implement joining through a server-side action, following the repository's
  existing Server Action conventions.
  5. Require a verified authenticated session inside the action, regardless of
  proxy behavior.
  6. Validate the submitted community ID using Zod.
  7. Derive the user ID exclusively from the authenticated session.
  8. Confirm the community exists before creating a membership.
  9. Create the membership with role MEMBER only. Never accept a role or userId
  from the client.
  10. Use the existing unique (userId, communityId) constraint to handle
  duplicate or concurrent join attempts safely.
  11. Make repeat joins idempotent without changing an existing ADMIN
  membership into MEMBER.
  12. Do not expose internal database errors or sensitive information to the
  user.
  13. After successful joining, refresh the community list and provide a clear
  way to open the newly joined community's listings.
  14. Preserve the existing Your Communities list, ADMIN indicators, sign-out
  functionality, and community-specific listings.
  15. Ensure all discovery and joining operations are server-side and avoid
  caching user-specific membership state.

  SECURITY REQUIREMENTS

  - A user must never be able to create a membership for another user.
  - A user must never be able to assign themselves ADMIN.
  - A nonexistent or invalid community must not create a membership.
  - Duplicate submissions must not create duplicate rows.
  - Concurrent joins must be safe.
  - Existing ADMIN memberships must remain unchanged.
  - Community discovery should expose only the minimal fields needed for open
  joining.
  - Validate Server Action inputs; do not rely on UI validation alone.
  - Avoid logging secrets or sensitive user information.

  TESTING

  Add focused unit tests and local PostgreSQL integration tests for:

  1. Discovering available communities.
  2. Excluding communities the current user has already joined.
  3. Joining an existing community.
  4. Newly joined communities appearing in Your Communities.
  5. Immediate access to that community's published listings.
  6. No access to listings in other communities.
  7. Invalid community IDs.
  8. Nonexistent communities.
  9. Unauthenticated join attempts.
  10. Duplicate joins.
  11. Concurrent join attempts.
  12. Preserving existing ADMIN roles.
  13. Rejection or safe ignoring of client-supplied userId and role.
  14. Appropriate error handling and safe UI rendering.

  Use the existing isolated PGlite server on 127.0.0.1:54329. Verify the
  listener before database testing, and use local-db-guard.mjs for every
  database operation. Never access shared Neon.

  VALIDATION

  - Focused unit and database tests.
  - Complete Vitest suite against local PostgreSQL using --no-file-parallelism.
  - CI-style test suite with database variables removed.
  - npm run lint.
  - npx next typegen.
  - npx tsc --noEmit.
  - npm run build with DATABASE_URL and TEST_DATABASE_URL empty.
  - git diff --check.
  - Report exact passing, failing, and skipped test counts.

  STRICT SCOPE

  - Do not modify prisma/schema.prisma.
  - Do not create or modify migrations.
  - Do not modify prisma/seed.ts.
  - Do not change existing database models or relationships.
  - Do not install packages.
  - Do not implement invitations, private communities, approvals, or community
  creation.
  - Do not implement unrelated user stories.
  - Do not modify existing Auth.js or proxy security logic unless you identify
  a genuine blocker and stop for review.
  - Do not read or modify .env files or real credentials.
  - Do not access shared Neon.
  - Do not perform Git staging, commits, pushes, merges, resets, or branch
  changes.
  - Leave all changes uncommitted.

  IMPORTANT:
  Do not automatically run a real Google OAuth login. That will be handled
  separately during final verification.

  FINAL REPORT

  1. Files changed.
  2. How discovery and joining work.
  3. How the Server Action enforces authenticated identity and MEMBER-only
  creation.
  4. How duplicate and concurrent joins are handled.
  5. How joining immediately enables community-scoped listings.
  6. Exact unit and real-database test results.
  7. Lint, TypeScript, build, and Git checks.
  8. Any remaining US-1 acceptance criteria or risks.

  Implement and test the complete open-joining workflow in this session. Stop
  only if a genuine architectural or security blocker appears. Leave the
  implementation uncommitted for my review.

       85 +/** A community open to join: only what the Discover list need
          +s. */
       86 +export type JoinableCommunity = { id: string; name: string; de
          +scription: string | null };
       87 +
       88 +/**
       89 + * Communities `user` hasn't joined, sorted by name.
       90 + *
       91 + * Communities are open in v1 (decision D-1): any signed-in us
          +er may discover
       92 + * and join any community. Only the name and description are e
          +xposed, never
       93 + * members, roles, or listings.
       94 + */
       95 +export async function listJoinableCommunities(
       96 +  user: AuthenticatedUser,
       97 +  getClient: () => CommunityMembershipDb = getDb,
       98 +): Promise<JoinableCommunity[]> {
       99 +  return getClient().community.findMany({
      100 +    where: { memberships: { none: { userId: user.id } } },
      101 +    select: { id: true, name: true, description: true },
      102 +    orderBy: [{ name: "asc" }, { id: "asc" }],
      103 +  });
      104 +}
      105 +
      106 +export type JoinCommunityResult = "joined" | "already_member"
          +| "not_found";
      107 +
      108 +/** Prisma's error code for a foreign key violation. */
      109 +const FOREIGN_KEY_VIOLATION = "P2003";
      110 +
      111 +function isForeignKeyViolation(error: unknown): boolean {
      112 +  return typeof error === "object" && error !== null && "code"
          + in error && error.code === FOREIGN_KEY_VIOLATION;
      113 +}
      114 +
      115 +/**
      116 + * Adds `user` to a community as a MEMBER.
      117 + *
      118 + * - `user` must come from the server session; the role is alw
          +ays MEMBER.
      119 + * - A missing community creates nothing (`not_found`).
      120 + * - The insert is `INSERT … ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` on the (u
          +serId,
      121 + *   communityId) unique key (`skipDuplicates`), so duplicate
          +or concurrent
      122 + *   joins can't create a second row or fail, and an existing
          +membership,
      123 + *   including an ADMIN one, is never modified (`already_membe
          +r`).
      124 + * - If the community is deleted between the check and the ins
          +ert, the foreign
      125 + *   key rejects the row and the result is `not_found`.
      126 + */
      127 +export async function joinCommunityAsMember(
      128 +  user: AuthenticatedUser,
      129 +  communityId: string,
      130 +  getClient: () => CommunityMembershipDb = getDb,
      131 +): Promise<JoinCommunityResult> {
      132 +  const db = getClient();
      133 +  const community = await db.community.findUnique({ where: { i
          +d: communityId }, select: { id: true } });
      134 +  if (!community) {
      135 +    return "not_found";
      136 +  }
      137 +  try {
      138 +    const { count } = await db.membership.createMany({
      139 +      data: [{ userId: user.id, communityId: community.id, rol
          +e: "MEMBER" }],
      140 +      skipDuplicates: true,
      141 +    });
      142 +    return count === 1 ? "joined" : "already_member";
      143 +  } catch (error) {
      144 +    if (isForeignKeyViolation(error)) {
      145 +      return "not_found";
      146 +    }
      147 +    throw error;
      148 +  }
      149 +}
      150 +
      151  // Compile-time contract: Prisma's CommunityRole enum and the
           role type used by
      152  // the pure access rules must stay identical. A new or renamed
            role in either
      153  // place fails `tsc` here.

      19 +/**

         +rything itself:
      23 + * - the session is verified here (`requireUser`); signed-out c
         +allers are
      24 + *   redirected to sign-in before anything else runs;
      25 + * - only the `communityId` field is read, and it's validated w
         +ith Zod. Any
      26 + *   other submitted field (for example `userId` or `role`) is
         +never read;
      27 + * - the user ID comes from the session, and the role is always
         + MEMBER;
      28 + * - errors are reported with one generic message, never databa
         +se details.
      29 + */
      30 +export async function joinCommunityAction(
      31 +  _previousState: JoinCommunityState,
      32 +  formData: FormData,
      33 +): Promise<JoinCommunityState> {
      34 +  const user = await requireUser(COMMUNITIES_PATH);
      35 +
      36 +  const parsed = communityIdSchema.safeParse(formData.get("comm
         +unityId"));
      37 +  if (!parsed.success) {

      41 +  let result;

      43 +    result = await joinCommunityAsMember({ id: user.id }, parse

      44 +  } catch {
      45 +    return { status: "error", message: JOIN_FAILED_MESSAGE };
      46 +  }
      47 +  if (result === "not_found") {
      48 +    return { status: "error", message: JOIN_FAILED_MESSAGE };
      49 +  }
      50 +
      51 +  // "joined" and "already_member" both mean the user is now a
         +member.
      52 +  refresh();

  The full suite passed against the local database: 453 passed, 0 failed, 0

  There were no schema, migration, seed, proxy, Auth.js, package, env or Git
  changes, and every database command went through local-db-guard.mjs.

  Your decision settles an open item in the docs. docs/PRD.md lists the joining
  mechanism as ⚠ Needs team decision, and architecture decision D4 in §12 is
  "Team" and undecided. Nothing in the repo requires invitation-only or
  approval-based communities, and listings stay members-only, so there was no
  conflict to stop for. D4 in docs/architecture.md §12 and the open question
  under US-1 in the PRD should now record "open joining (v1)". I didn't edit the
  docs, since that's outside this task's scope.

  1. Files changed

  File: src/server/dal/memberships.ts
  Change: Added listJoinableCommunities, joinCommunityAsMember, the
    JoinableCommunity and JoinCommunityResult types, and the
    CommunityMembershipDb delegate type
  ────────────────────────────────────────

    signOutAction is unchanged

    a "Join community" / "Joining…" button, and an error message
    (role="alert")

  Change: Loads both lists in parallel and adds the "Discover communities"
    section. Each of your communities now also has a "View listings →" link.
    The empty-state text now reads "Join one below…"
  ────────────────────────────────────────
  File: Tests
  Change: communities.test.ts (+7 Discover tests; two existing assertions
    adjusted for the extra link and the new section), memberships.test.ts
    (+6), join-action.test.ts (new, 11), join-community-form.test.ts (new, 2),
    prisma/joining.test.ts (new, 13 database tests)

  2. How discovery and joining work

  - Discovery: listJoinableCommunities(user) runs community.findMany({ where: {
    memberships: { none: { userId: user.id } } }, select: { id, name,
    description }, orderBy: name }). It shows only what a Join button needs: no
    members, roles, counts or listings. Communities you haven't joined aren't
    linked, so their listings stay hidden until you join.
  - The page: /communities checks the session first (connection(), then
    requireUser), then loads your communities and the joinable ones together.
    Both reads are per user, on every request, with no "use cache".
  - Joining: the form posts to joinCommunityAction. On success the action calls
    refresh(), which the bundled docs specify for Server Actions. The page
    re-renders with the community moved into Your communities, where it has a
    "View listings →" link.

  3. How the Server Action enforces identity and MEMBER-only joins

  - requireUser("/communities") runs first, inside the action, whatever the
    proxy did. A signed-out caller is redirected to sign-in before the form is
    read.
  - Only formData.get("communityId") is read, and it's validated with the
    existing communityIdSchema. userId, role and any other submitted fields are
    never read.
  - The user ID comes from the session, and the role is hard-coded to MEMBER in
    the insert, so it's impossible to join on someone else's behalf or as an
    admin.
  - joinCommunityAsMember checks the community exists with findUnique before
    inserting. Nonexistent communities and invalid IDs create nothing.
  - Every failure returns one generic message: "We couldn't join that community.
    Refresh the page and try again." That covers invalid input, a missing
    community, and any unexpected database error. Nothing is logged and no
    details are exposed; a test feeds in an error containing ECONNREFUSED …
    password= and confirms none of it reaches the response.

  4. How duplicate and concurrent joins are handled

  - The insert is membership.createMany({ data: [{ userId, communityId, role:
    "MEMBER" }], skipDuplicates: true }). On PostgreSQL that becomes INSERT … ON
    CONFLICT DO NOTHING on the existing unique (userId, communityId) key.
    - It returns count: 1 for a new join and count: 0 if the membership already
      exists.
    - It never raises a duplicate-key error, and never changes an existing row,
      so an ADMIN stays ADMIN.
  - Repeat joins are idempotent: "already a member" counts as success.
  - A deletion race is handled: if the community is deleted between the
    existence check and the insert, the foreign-key error (P2003) becomes "not
    found". Any other error is rethrown and shown as the generic message.

  Concurrency, and the limit of what I tested:
  - What the test proves: 10 Server Action calls in flight at once all returned
    "joined", and exactly one row exists.
  - What went wrong on the way: the local PGlite server closes extra
    simultaneous connections (P1017 ConnectionClosed). I reproduced that
    separately; the join logic itself was correct in every run, with one insert,
    the rest skipped and never a duplicate.
  - The adjustment: the joining test file now uses a 2-connection pool.
  - What it doesn't prove: PGlite runs statements one at a time, so this isn't a
    truly parallel race. Protection against real races rests on PostgreSQL's ON
    CONFLICT on the unique index, which a real-Postgres CI job could confirm.

  5. Why joining immediately grants access to listings

  Joining creates the Membership row that every access check already reads from
  the database on each request, through the CP-10 proxy and the CP-9 page.
  Nothing is cached in the JWT or anywhere else, so access changes on the very
  next request. The database test confirms this for dave and Alpha:

  │ joining       │                          │                             │

  Risks and follow-ups:
  1. Concurrency on real PostgreSQL is covered by design, not by a test
     (section 4). A CI job with a Postgres service container would close that
     gap.
  2. The docs still show D4 as open (see the top of this report).
  3. Anyone signed in can join any community. That's your intended v1 decision,
     but it means community isolation now protects non-members, not communities
     from outsiders. Private communities, invites and abuse controls are out of
     scope.
  4. The Join action has no rate limit. It's idempotent and cheap, but per-user
     rate limiting is still undecided in docs/security.md.
  5. Still open from earlier: the scratchpad HTTP check isn't part of CI, and
     the proxy's database lookup on /c/* requests is a performance trade-off
     against Next.js guidance.

  Suggested commit message: feat(communities): let signed-in users discover and
  join open communities

~~~~

**Annotation (my approach):** The joining flow was one of the last pieces. Some database checks failed along the way, but we traced that to the local PGlite server rather than immediately changing the app. Once the test server was sorted out, we reran the checks. It was a useful lesson in not assuming every failing test means the code is wrong.

~~~~text
  One failed run during this audit was the test server, not the code. The first
  full-suite run failed 41 database tests with P1017 ConnectionClosed: the
  long-running local PGlite server (up since CP-7B) had started dropping
  simultaneous connections. I restarted it with the same data folder (still
  127.0.0.1:54329 only, both migrations still applied), and the suite then
  passed 453/453 twice. PGlite's connection handling is a weak spot in the test
  setup. A real Postgres in CI would remove it.

  The Join action and real HTTP (item 8)

  - Now verified over real HTTP: the no-JavaScript form submission through the
    actual stack: proxy, session cookie, Next's Origin (CSRF) check, the Server
    Action and the database.
  - Still unverified:
    a. The JavaScript path in a real browser: useActionState posting with Next's
       internal Next-Action header, the "Joining…" pending state, refresh()
       updating the page in place, and the error message display. No browser
       automation is installed (Playwright is planned but not added).
    b. A note for the team: a cross-origin Server Action POST is rejected with
       HTTP 500 ("Invalid Server Actions request"), not a 4xx. That's Next.js's
       own behavior. It's safe, but it shows up as a server error in logs.

  Google OAuth: manual steps (item 9)

  The app reads AUTH_SECRET, AUTH_GOOGLE_ID, AUTH_GOOGLE_SECRET, AUTH_URL (or
  AUTH_TRUST_HOST=true when running outside Vercel) and DATABASE_URL. I read no
  env files.

  1. Google Cloud Console, OAuth consent screen: choose External, set the app
     name and support email, and request the scopes openid, email and profile.
     While it's in Testing mode, add the Google accounts that will test it.
  2. Credentials, create an OAuth client ID of type Web application:
     - Authorized JavaScript origins: http://localhost:3000, plus your Vercel
       URL later.
     - Authorized redirect URIs: http://localhost:3000/api/auth/callback/google,
       plus https://<vercel-domain>/api/auth/callback/google later.
  3. Put the credentials in your gitignored .env.local: AUTH_GOOGLE_ID,
     AUTH_GOOGLE_SECRET, and an AUTH_SECRET from npx auth secret or openssl rand
     -base64 32. Never commit them. Add the same values in Vercel's settings
     later.
  4. This one blocks real sign-in: the database your dev server uses must
     contain the Account table. Two options:
     - Point DATABASE_URL at the local PGlite server for testing.
     - Apply 20261122120000_add_auth_account to the shared database with prisma
       migrate deploy. That needs team approval; I never touched Neon.

     Without the table, sign-in fails with an Auth.js adapter error.
  5. Check in a browser with npm run dev:
     a. Sign in with a non-university Google account. You should land on
        /communities.
     b. Sign out and back in. Prisma Studio should show 1 User and 1 Account,
        with the token columns empty.
     c. Join a community from the Discover list. The button should show
        "Joining…", and the community should move into Your communities.
     d. Open "View listings →". Only that community's published items should
        appear.
     e. Open /c/<id>/admin. You should get a 403.
     f. Sign out, then open /communities. You should be redirected to sign-in.
~~~~

**Annotation (my approach):** At the end, the session reported 453 passing tests, 26 production HTTP authorization checks, 15 joining checks, and 96.17% statement coverage. I also kept the limitations in the report. A real Google sign-in still needed a browser check with credentials at that point, and the existing CI workflow wasn't running the full test suite. I didn't want to make the results sound more complete than they were.


## 10. What I learned from this workflow

I found Explore → Plan → Implement → Commit useful because it made me check what Claude was doing at each stage. Explore helped us find the existing code, Plan Mode brought up dependencies I might have overlooked, and the smaller checkpoints made the changes easier to review. I also did the Git commits myself instead of letting Claude make a bunch of changes and commit everything at once.

The things I remember most are catching the encoded URL issue before committing, finding out why a page could show an error but still return HTTP 200, and having to change how we ran local database tests. Those were all cases where checking the details mattered more than just getting another green test result.

## Evidence boundaries and companion links

- **Commits:** The Claude export contains checkpoint reports and suggested commit messages, not the human terminal's full Git command history. Use [PR #37's commit history](https://github.com/aarushikaushikk/BorrowLoop/pull/37/commits) to verify meaningful commits.
- **Part 1:** `logs/claude-setup.md` contains the earlier `/init`, root `CLAUDE.md`, modular docs, imports, permissions, and CI transcripts.
- **Context compaction:** This US-1 export reports automatic compaction. It does **not** establish that I manually ran `/compact`, `/clear`, or `--continue`.
- **Strict TDD:** Some US-1 checkpoints used test-first development, but this document is the Part 2 Explore → Plan → Implement → Commit evidence. It is not a substitute for the separate strict RED → GREEN → REFACTOR deliverable.
- **CI scope:** The initial workflow checked install, lint, type generation, type-check, and build. It did not run the full unit/integration suite at the time described in this transcript. Any later CI changes must be evidenced separately.
- **Original source:** This document reproduces the exported session, including collapsed tool calls. It does not reconstruct messages from earlier sessions that were not in the export.
